Chapter 0242: Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

067_Holomorphic | 1001 Theorems and Proofs
« Previous Index Next »

Table of Contents

Theorems 1-1001 on Mendelian Inheritance Patterns in 067_Holomorphic.

Theorem 0001 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 1. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0002 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 2. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0003 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 3. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Theorem 0040 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 40. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. Theorem 79. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 118. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 157. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 196. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 235. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 274. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 313. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 352. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 391. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. Theorem 430. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 469. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 508. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 547. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 586. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Theorem 625. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Theorem 0664 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 664. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0665 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 665. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Theorem 0703 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 703. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0704 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 704. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0742 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0780 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 780. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Theorem 0781 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 781. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0819 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 819. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Theorem 0820 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 820. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0859 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 859. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0896 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 896. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0897 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 897. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0935 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 935. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0936 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 936. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.

Proof. Define the measure $\nu(E) = \int_E f\,d\mu$ for $f \in L^1(\mu)$, $f \geq 0$. By definition $\nu$ is a non-negative measure on $(X, \mathcal{F})$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The Radon-Nikodym theorem provides the density: $d\nu/d\mu = f$ $\mu$-a.e. For any $\sigma$-finite submeasure $\mu_0 \leq \mu$, the restricted density $f|_{\text{supp}(\mu_0)}$ remains in $L^1(\mu_0)$, preserving the integral representation.

Theorem 0974 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 974. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. A continuous-time Markov chain $(X_t)_{t \geq 0}$ on state space $S$ has transition rates $q_{ij} \geq 0$ for $i \neq j$ and $q_{ii} = -\sum_{j \neq i} q_{ij}$. The Kolmogorov forward equation is $\dot{P}(t) = P(t)Q$. Stationary distribution $\pi$ satisfies $\pi Q = 0$ with $\sum_i \pi_i = 1$. For irreducible chains, $\pi_i = \frac{1}{m_{ii} r_i}$ where $m_{ii}$ is the mean return time and $r_i$ is the rate out of state $i$.

Theorem 0975 Mendelian Inheritance Patterns

Theorem 975. In the context of Mendelian genetics, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Riemann zeta function $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} n^{-s}$ for $\text{Re}(s) > 1$ extends to a meromorphic function on $\mathbb{C}$ with a simple pole at $s = 1$. The functional equation $\zeta(s) = 2^s \pi^{s-1} \sin(\pi s/2) \Gamma(1-s) \zeta(1-s)$ connects values at $s$ and $1-s$. The Euler product $\zeta(s) = \prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1}$ establishes the link to primes, and $\zeta(2) = \pi^2/6$ by Euler's Basel computation.
